[Presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_election) were held in the [United States](3434750.md) on November 5, 2024. The [Republican](32070.md) ticket of former [president](24113.md) [Donald Trump](4848272.md) and [Ohio](22199.md) junior [senator](24909346.md) [JD Vance](53396477.md) defeated the [Democratic](5043544.md) ticket of incumbent [vice president](32759.md) [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) and [Minnesota governor](214877.md) [Tim Walz](2216593.md).

The incumbent president, [Joe Biden](145422.md) of the Democratic Party, initially [ran for re-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_2024_presidential_campaign),[^cite_note-3] easily sweeping aside a challenge from Democratic [representative](19468510.md) [Dean Phillips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dean_Phillips) of Minnesota in [the Democratic primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries) to become the party's [presumptive nominee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presumptive_nominee_\(United_States\)).[^cite_note-4][^cite_note-5] However, what was broadly considered a [poor debate performance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Joe_Biden%E2%80%93Donald_Trump_presidential_debate) in June 2024 intensified [concerns about his age and health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_and_health_concerns_about_Joe_Biden), and led to [calls within his party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Democrats_who_opposed_the_Joe_Biden_2024_presidential_campaign) for him to leave the race.[^cite_note-6] After initially declining to do so, Biden ultimately [withdrew from the race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_Joe_Biden_from_the_2024_United_States_presidential_election) on July 21, 2024, becoming the first eligible incumbent president to run but subsequently withdraw since [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) in [1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_Lyndon_B._Johnson_from_the_1968_United_States_presidential_election).[^cite_note-7] Biden immediately endorsed Harris,[^cite_note-NBC_Timeline-8] who officially became [the party's presidential nominee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris_2024_presidential_campaign) on August 5 and the first presidential nominee who did not participate in the [primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_primary) since Vice President [Hubert Humphrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubert_Humphrey) in [1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubert_Humphrey_1968_presidential_campaign). Harris selected Walz as [her running mate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Democratic_Party_vice_presidential_candidate_selection).[^cite_note-9][^cite_note-10]

Trump, who lost [the 2020 presidential election](48410011.md) to Biden, [ran for reelection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2024_presidential_campaign) to a nonconsecutive second term. Trump became the presumptive Republican Party's presidential nominee on March 12, 2024, easily defeating challengers such as former [South Carolina](27956.md) [governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_\(United_States\)) [Nikki Haley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikki_Haley) and [Florida](18933066.md) governor [Ron DeSantis](36729527.md) in the [Republican primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries).[^cite_note-11] He was shot in the ear in [an assassination attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attempted_assassination_of_Donald_Trump_in_Pennsylvania) on July 13, 2024. Trump was nominated as the Republican Party's presidential candidate during the [2024 Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Republican_National_Convention) alongside [his running mate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Republican_Party_vice_presidential_candidate_selection), JD Vance. The Trump campaign ticket supported mass deportation of [illegal immigrants](7124412.md);[^cite_note-13] an [isolationist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isolationism) "[America First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America_First)" and "[peace through strength](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_through_strength)" foreign policy agenda with support of [Israel](9282173.md) in the [Gaza war](74998519.md) and skepticism of [Ukraine](31750.md) in [its war](70149799.md) with [Russia](25391.md); policies [hostile to transgender Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transphobia_in_the_United_States); and [greatly increased import tariffs](78448911.md). The campaign also made [false and misleading statements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_or_misleading_statements_by_Donald_Trump), including [claims of electoral fraud in 2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_claims_of_fraud_in_the_2020_presidential_election). Historians and some former [Trump administrators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_cabinet_of_Donald_Trump) saw [his political movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trumpism) as [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian).

Trump won the election over Harris, with 312 [Electoral College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Electoral_College) votes to Harris's 226.[^cite_note-Results-14] Trump won all of the top seven [swing states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swing_state), including [Nevada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_States_presidential_election_in_Nevada) for the Republicans' first time since [2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_United_States_presidential_election_in_Nevada). Trump won the national [popular vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_United_States_presidential_elections_by_popular_vote_margin) with a [plurality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plurality_\(voting\)) of 49.8%, making him the first Republican to win the popular vote since [George W. Bush](3414021.md) in [2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_United_States_presidential_election) (unlike his [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election) victory and [2020](48410011.md) defeat). Trump's victory made him the second U.S. president to be elected to a nonconsecutive second term, after [Grover Cleveland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grover_Cleveland) in [1892](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1892_United_States_presidential_election). Surveys of 2024 election voters, nationally and in key states, found that many viewed [economic conditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_affordability_crisis) negatively and were motivated by the issue when they voted. Other issues that motivated voters included immigration, the state of democracy, and abortion.[^cite_note-15][^cite_note-16][^cite_note-17][^cite_note-FoxVoter-18]

## Background

Further information: [United States presidential election § Procedure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_election#Procedure)



[Joe Biden](145422.md), the incumbent president in 2024, whose term expired at noon on January 20, 2025



A general election absentee ballot from [Fairfax County, Virginia](91327.md), listing the presidential and vice presidential candidates

In 2020, incumbent Republican President Donald Trump sought re-election but was defeated by Democratic challenger Joe Biden. Democratic U.S. Senator Kamala Harris of California was elected vice president in 2020 as Biden's running mate.[^cite_note-19]

Trump is the first president in American history to be [impeached twice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efforts_to_impeach_Donald_Trump) and run for president again after impeachment. As Trump was acquitted by the Senate in both cases, he was not barred from seeking reelection to the presidency in 2024.[^cite_note-20]

### Election interference

Main article: [Election interference](65542411.md)

Further information: [Republican efforts to restrict voting following the 2020 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_efforts_to_restrict_voting_following_the_2020_presidential_election), [Attempts to overturn the 2020 United States presidential election](65905202.md), [January 6 United States Capitol attack](66307490.md), and [Presidential eligibility of Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidential_eligibility_of_Donald_Trump)

Several state courts and officials, including the [Colorado Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colorado_Supreme_Court),[^cite_note-21] a state Circuit Court in [Illinois](14849.md),[^cite_note-22] and the [Secretary of State of Maine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_of_Maine),[^cite_note-Kazarian,_Grace-2023-23] ruled that [Trump was ineligible to hold office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_presidential_eligibility_of_Donald_Trump) under Section 3 of the [Fourteenth Amendment to the United States Constitution](31666.md) for his role in the [January 6 Capitol attack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/January_6_Capitol_attack), and thus attempted to disqualify him from appearing on the ballot.[^cite_note-24][^cite_note-Kazarian,_Grace-2023-23] These attempts were unsuccessful. On March 4, 2024, the U.S. Supreme Court unanimously ruled in *[Trump v. Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_v._Anderson)* that states cannot determine eligibility for a national election under Section 3. The Court held that only Congress has the authority to disqualify candidates, or to pass legislation that allows courts to do so.[^cite_note-25]

#### Donald Trump's false claims of interference

Further information: [Big lie § Donald Trump's false claims of a stolen election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_lie#Donald_Trump's_false_claims_of_a_stolen_election), [Election denial movement in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_denial_movement_in_the_United_States), and [Republican Party efforts to disrupt the 2024 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_efforts_to_disrupt_the_2024_United_States_presidential_election)



To sow election doubt, Trump escalated use of "rigged election" and "election interference" statements in advance of the 2024 election compared to the previous two elections.

[^cite_note-The_New_York_Times-2024-6-26]

Trump made [false claims of voter fraud in the 2020 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_claims_of_voter_fraud_in_the_2020_presidential_election) and denied the validity of the election results.[^cite_note-27][^cite_note-28] In July 2024, *The New York Times* reported that "the Republican Party and its conservative allies are engaged in an unprecedented legal campaign targeting the American voting system", by restricting voting for partisan advantage ahead of Election Day and preparing to mount "legally dubious" challenges against the certification process if Trump were to lose.[^cite_note-29]

In the lead-up to the 2024 election, the Republican Party made false claims of massive "noncitizen voting" by immigrants in an attempt to delegitimize the election in the event of a Trump defeat.[^cite_note-30][^cite_note-31][^cite_note-32] The claims were made as part of larger efforts within the Republican Party to disrupt the 2024 election and promote [election denial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_denial).[^cite_note-33] Trump and several other Republicans stated that they would not accept the results of the 2024 election if they believed they were "unfair".[^cite_note-34]

Trump's previous comments suggesting he could "terminate" the [Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Constitution) to reverse his election loss,[^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36] his claim that he would only be a dictator on "day one" of his presidency and not afterwards,[^cite_note-37] his promise to use the Justice Department to go after his political enemies,[^cite_note-38] his plan to use the [Insurrection Act of 1807](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insurrection_Act_of_1807) to deploy the military for law enforcement in primarily Democratic cities and states,[^cite_note-39][^cite_note-40] [attempts to overturn the 2020 United States presidential election](65905202.md), his baseless predictions of voter fraud in the 2024 election,[^cite_note-41] and his public embrace and celebration of the [January 6 United States Capitol attack](66307490.md),[^cite_note-42] raised [concerns over the state of democracy in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concerns_over_the_state_of_democracy_in_the_United_States).[^cite_note-trump-dictatorship-43][^cite_note-44][^cite_note-45][^cite_note-46] Trump's political operation said that it planned to deploy more than 100,000 attorneys and volunteers to polling places across battleground states, with an "election integrity hotline" for poll watchers and voters to report alleged voting irregularities.[^cite_note-47]

#### Interference by foreign nations

Main article: [Foreign interference in the 2024 United States elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_interference_in_the_2024_United_States_elections)

Before the election, U.S. officials and former officials stated that foreign interference in the 2024 election was likely. Three major factors cited were "America's deepening domestic political crises, the collapse of controversial attempts to control political speech on social media, and the rise of [generative AI](73291755.md)".[^cite_note-48] China, Russia, and Iran were identified as mounting influence operations and attempts to interfere with the 2024 election. U.S. intelligence officials described the efforts as part of broader efforts by authoritarian nations to use the internet to erode support for democracy.[^cite_note-Klepper_09032024-49]

##### China

Further information: [Chinese interference in the 2024 United States elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_interference_in_the_2024_United_States_elections)

China was identified as interfering with the 2024 election through propaganda and disinformation campaigns linked to its [Spamouflage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spamouflage) operation. U.S. intelligence agencies described the effort as not targeting any particular candidate but focusing on issues important to the [Chinese government](23239.md), such as Taiwan, and "undermining confidence in elections, voting, and the U.S. in general".[^cite_note-Klepper_09032024-49] As early as April 1, 2024, *The New York Times* reported that the Chinese government had created fake pro-Trump accounts on social media "promoting conspiracy theories, stoking domestic divisions and attacking President Biden ahead of the election in November".[^cite_note-50]

##### Russia

Further information: [Russian interference in the 2024 United States elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_interference_in_the_2024_United_States_elections)

According to disinformation experts and intelligence agencies, Russia spread disinformation ahead of the 2024 election to damage Biden and Democrats, boost candidates supporting isolationism, and undercut support for Ukraine aid and NATO.[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52] On September 4, 2024, the United States publicly accused Russia of interfering in the 2024 election and announced several steps to combat Russian influence including [sanctions](70323386.md), indictments, and seizing of web domains used to spread propaganda and disinformation. U.S. intelligence agencies assessed that Russia preferred Trump to win the election, viewing him as more critical of American support for Ukraine.[^cite_note-53]

##### Iran

Further information: [Iranian interference in the 2024 United States elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_interference_in_the_2024_United_States_elections)

The Islamic Republic of Iran was identified as interfering with the 2024 presidential election through front companies connected to the [Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps](1285472.md) and hacking attempts against the Trump, Biden, and Harris campaigns starting as early as May 2024.[^cite_note-USA_Today-2024-54] The Iranian regime launched propaganda and disinformation campaigns through fake news websites and accounts on social media to tip the election against former president Trump. *The New York Times* stated the efforts were an attempt at "sowing internal discord and discrediting the democratic system in the United States more broadly in the eyes of the world."[^cite_note-USA_Today-2024-54][^cite_note-55][^cite_note-56]

### Voter roll purges

Further information: [Republican efforts to restrict voting following the 2020 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_efforts_to_restrict_voting_following_the_2020_presidential_election)

Multiple Republican-led administrations removed voters from their states' voter rolls in the lead up to the election, which critics argued violates the [National Voter Registration Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Voter_Registration_Act_of_1993).[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-guardianaug30-58][^cite_note-59] In July 2024, 160,000 inactive or infrequent voters were removed from [Ohio](22199.md)'s voter rolls.[^cite_note-60][^cite_note-61] The Ohio chapters of [Common Cause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Cause) and the [League of Women Voters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Women_Voters) threatened lawsuits against the state over the purge.[^cite_note-62][^cite_note-63]

In August 2024, Governor [Glenn Youngkin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glenn_Youngkin) of [Virginia](32432.md) signed an executive order removing 6,303 voters suspected of being non-citizens from Virginia's voter rolls.[^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65] In October 2024, the [U.S. Department of Justice](52563.md) sued the Virginia Board of Elections and Virginia commissioner of elections over the voter purge, alleging that it violated the [National Voter Registration Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Voter_Registration_Act).[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-JD-67] The suit also found a number of alleged non-citizens purged were actually citizens.[^cite_note-JD-67][^cite_note-AC-68] District judge [Patricia Tolliver Giles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patricia_Tolliver_Giles) ruled that the removal was illegal, ordering the state to stop purging voter rolls and to restore the voter registration of more than 1,600 voters who had been removed.[^cite_note-69][^cite_note-AC-68] The [4th Circuit Court of Appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/4th_Circuit_Court_of_Appeals) then upheld the order.[^cite_note-70][^cite_note-CP-71] The administration filed an emergency appeal to the [U.S. Supreme Court](31737.md), which sided with Virginia in a 6–3 decision [along ideological lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ideological_leanings_of_United_States_Supreme_Court_justices), allowing the state to continue purging voter rolls.[^cite_note-CP-71][^cite_note-72]

In August 2024, [Alabama](303.md) Secretary of State [Wes Allen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wes_Allen_\(politician\)) announced a process for purging 3,251 registered Alabama voters and referred them to the state attorney general's office for criminal prosecution.[^cite_note-guardianaug30-58][^cite_note-73] In September 2024, the Department of Justice sued Alabama for violating the National Voter Registration Act.[^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75] In October 2024, district judge [Anna Manasco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anna_Manasco) ruled in favor of the Department of Justice, ordering the state to restore the voter registrations.[^cite_note-76][^cite_note-FJ-77] Alabama secretary of state's chief of staff Clay Helms testified that 2,000 of the purged voters were legally registered citizens.[^cite_note-FJ-77]

### Criminal and civil legal proceedings involving Donald Trump

Main article: [Indictments against Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indictments_against_Donald_Trump)

Further information: [Federal prosecution of Donald Trump (election obstruction case)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_prosecution_of_Donald_Trump_\(election_obstruction_case\)), [Georgia election racketeering prosecution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgia_election_racketeering_prosecution), [Federal prosecution of Donald Trump (classified documents case)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_prosecution_of_Donald_Trump_\(classified_documents_case\)), [Prosecution of Donald Trump in New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prosecution_of_Donald_Trump_in_New_York), and [Personal and business legal affairs of Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_and_business_legal_affairs_of_Donald_Trump)

Trump was the subject of various criminal and civil legal proceedings before and during his 2024 re-election campaign. Specifically, Trump was found liable in a civil proceeding for [financial fraud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_fraud) in 2023,[^cite_note-hushhush-78] was found liable for both [sexual abuse](7397019.md) and [defamation](28661.md) in 2023, and was found liable for defamation in a related civil proceeding in 2024. In 2024, Trump was criminally convicted of 34 [felonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felonies) related to [falsifying business records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifying_business_records).[^cite_note-79] Trump and other Republicans made numerous false and misleading statements regarding Trump's various legal proceedings, including false claims that they were "rigged" or consisted of "election interference" orchestrated by Biden and the Democratic Party.[^cite_note-The_New_York_Times-2024-4-80][^cite_note-The_New_York_Times-2024-6-26]



Classified intelligence material found inside [Mar-a-Lago](1452754.md) in 2022

On May 30, 2024, Trump was found guilty by a jury of all 34 felony counts in *[The People of the State of New York v. Donald J. Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_People_of_the_State_of_New_York_v._Donald_J._Trump)*. The jury found that Trump falsified business records relating to [hush money](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hush_money) payments made to pornographic film star [Stormy Daniels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stormy_Daniels) to ensure her silence about [a sexual encounter between them](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stormy_Daniels%E2%80%93Donald_Trump_scandal). This conviction made Trump the first former U.S. president to be convicted of a crime.[^cite_note-81] On January 10, 2025, Trump was given a no-penalty sentence known as an unconditional discharge.[^cite_note-82]

Trump faced other criminal charges as well. In *[United States of America v. Donald J. Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_prosecution_of_Donald_Trump_\(election_obstruction_case\))*, Trump faced four criminal counts for his alleged role in [attempting to overturn the 2020 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attempting_to_overturn_the_2020_United_States_presidential_election) and involvement in the [January 6 United States Capitol attack](66307490.md); the case was dismissed following Trump's re-election in November 2024.[^cite_note-83] In *[The State of Georgia v. Donald J. Trump, et al.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_State_of_Georgia_v._Donald_J._Trump,_et_al.)*, Trump was charged with eight criminal counts for his alleged attempts to overturn the results of the [2020 United States presidential election in Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_United_States_presidential_election_in_Georgia). District Attorney [Fani Willis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fani_Willis) was disqualified from prosecuting the case; Willis has appealed that decision.[^cite_note-84] In *United States of America v. Donald J. Trump, Waltine Nauta, and Carlos De Oliveira*, Trump faced 40 criminal counts relating to his [hoarding of classified documents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FBI_search_of_Mar-a-Lago) and alleged obstruction of efforts to retrieve them;[^cite_note-85] the case was dismissed in July 2024.[^cite_note-86]

On May 9, 2023, in *[E. Jean Carroll v. Donald J. Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E._Jean_Carroll_v._Donald_J._Trump)*, an anonymous jury found Trump civilly liable[^cite_note-87] for sexual abuse and defamation, and ordered him to pay Carroll \$5 million in damages.[^cite_note-88] In a related case brought by Carroll against Trump, a jury awarded Carroll \$83.3 million.[^cite_note-\:27-89] As of April 2025, appeals were ongoing in both cases.[^cite_note-90]

In September 2023, Trump was found civilly liable for [financial fraud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_fraud) in *[New York v. Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_business_fraud_lawsuit_against_the_Trump_Organization)*.[^cite_note-hushhush-78] In February 2024, he was ordered to pay a \$354.8 million fine, together with approximately \$100 million in interest.[^cite_note-91] As of January 29, 2025, an appeal was ongoing.[^cite_note-92]

Trump made efforts to delay his trials until after the 2024 election.[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-94] On July 1, 2024, the Supreme Court delivered a 6–3 decision in *[Trump v. United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_v._United_States_\(2024\))*, ruling that Trump had absolute immunity for acts he committed as president within his core constitutional purview, at least presumptive immunity for official acts within the outer perimeter of his official responsibility, and no immunity for unofficial acts.[^cite_note-95][^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97]

### Age and health concerns

#### Joe Biden

Main article: [Age and health concerns about Joe Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_and_health_concerns_about_Joe_Biden)

Mass media, lawmakers, and Donald Trump raised concerns about Biden's age, including his cognitive state, during and after the [2020 United States presidential election](48410011.md).[^cite_note-BAge-98] According to a February 2024 poll, Biden's age and health were major or moderate concerns for 86% of voters generally,[^cite_note-BAge-98] up from 76% in 2020.[^cite_note-99] According to another February 2024 poll, most of those who voted for Biden in 2020 believed he was too old to be an effective president; *[The New York Times](30680.md)* noted that these concerns "cut across generations, gender, race and education".[^cite_note-100]

Concerns about Biden's age and health increased after a poor performance by Biden during [a debate against Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Joe_Biden%E2%80%93Donald_Trump_presidential_debate) in June 2024. That performance led a number of commentators and Democratic lawmakers to call for Biden to drop out of the 2024 presidential race.[^cite_note-101] In July 2024, Biden ultimately [withdrew from the race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_Joe_Biden_from_the_2024_United_States_presidential_election) while stating that he would continue serving as president until the conclusion of his term.[^cite_note-wp-election-2024-102]

#### Donald Trump

Main article: [Age and health concerns about Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_and_health_concerns_about_Donald_Trump)

In the summer before the election, polling showed at least half of Americans thought that Trump, who was 78 years old, was too old to serve a second term, with 80% unsure he would be able to finish out a second term.[^cite_note-The_Independent-2024-103] Numerous public figures, media sources, and mental health professionals speculated that Trump may have some form of [dementia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dementia), which runs in his family.[^cite_note-wapojuly22-104] Experts for the science publication *[STAT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stat_\(website\))* who analyzed changes in Trump's speeches between 2015 and 2024 noted shorter sentences, more tangents, more repetition, and more confusion of words and phrases. Doctors suggested these changes could relate to Trump's moods or could indicate the beginning of [Alzheimer's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alzheimer's). One expert noted an increase in expressions of all-or-nothing thinking by Trump; a sharp rise in [all-or-nothing thinking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All-or-nothing_thinking) is also linked to [cognitive decline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_decline).[^cite_note-The_New_Republic-2024-105] *The New York Times* reported that Trump's 2024 speeches had grown "darker, harsher, longer, angrier, less focused, more profane and increasingly fixated on the past", and that experts considered this increase in [tangential speech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tangential_speech) and [behavioral disinhibition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Behavioral_disinhibition) as a possible consequence of advancing age and cognitive decline.[^cite_note-106] Trump was also criticized for his lack of transparency around his medical records and health.[^cite_note-wapojuly22-104][^cite_note-107]

### Violent rhetoric

Main article: [Political violence in the 2024 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_violence_in_the_2024_United_States_presidential_election)

See also: [Donald Trump 2024 presidential campaign § Violent and dehumanizing statements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_2024_presidential_campaign#Violent_and_dehumanizing_statements)

On July 14, Biden gave an address condemning political violence, including the attempted assassination of Trump, arguing for the need to "lower the temperature" in American politics.

Several scholars, lawmakers, intelligence agencies, and the members of the public expressed concerns about [political violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_violence) surrounding the 2024 election.[^cite_note-108][^cite_note-109] The fears came amidst increasing threats and acts of physical violence targeting public officials and election workers at all levels of government.[^cite_note-110][^cite_note-111] Trump was identified as a key figure in increasing political violence in the United States both for and against him.[^cite_note-112][^cite_note-Perspectives_on_Terrorism-2020-113][^cite_note-It's_About_Hate-2022-114] Political violence was at its highest since the 1970s, and the most recent violence came from right-wing assailants.[^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116] Trump increasingly embraced [far-right extremism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right_extremism), conspiracy theories such as [Q-Anon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Q-Anon), and far-right [militia movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_militia_movement) to a greater extent than any modern American president.[^cite_note-The_New_York_Times-2022-117][^cite_note-The_Associated_Press-2023-118] Trump also espoused dehumanizing, combative, and violent rhetoric,[^cite_note-119] and promised retribution against his political enemies.[^cite_note-vows-120] Trump played down but refused to rule out violence following the 2024 election, stating "it depends".[^cite_note-IbssaKim2024-121] Trump also suggested using the military against "the enemy from within" on Election Day that he described as "radical left lunatics", Democratic politicians, and those opposed to his candidacy.[^cite_note-122][^cite_note-Lerer_10152024-123]

## Nominations

### Republican Party

Main articles: [2024 Republican Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Republican_Party_presidential_primaries) and [2024 Republican Party presidential candidates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Republican_Party_presidential_candidates)



Results of the 2024 Republican presidential primaries. Trump (*blue*) won everything but [Vermont](32578.md) and [Washington D.C.](108956.md), which went to [Nikki Haley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikki_Haley) (*orange*).

Trump filed and announced his candidacy a week following the [2022 midterm elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_States_elections).[^cite_note-124] Trump was considered an early frontrunner for the Republican presidential nomination.[^cite_note-125] He had announced in March 2022 that his former vice president [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence) would not be his running mate.[^cite_note-126]

Trump faced opposition in the primaries. Florida Governor [Ron DeSantis](36729527.md) was initially viewed as the main challenger to Trump for the Republican nomination, having raised more campaign funds in the first half of 2022 and posting more favorable polling numbers than Trump by the end of 2022.[^cite_note-127][^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129] On May 24, 2023, DeSantis announced his candidacy on [Twitter](9988187.md) in an online conversation with the social media company's CEO, [Elon Musk](909036.md). At the end of July 2023, *[FiveThirtyEight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FiveThirtyEight)*'s national polling average of the Republican primaries had Trump at 52 percent, and DeSantis at 15.[^cite_note-130]

Following the [Iowa caucuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Iowa_Republican_presidential_caucuses), in which Trump posted a landslide victory, DeSantis and businessman [Vivek Ramaswamy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vivek_Ramaswamy) dropped out of the race and endorsed Trump, leaving the former president and [Nikki Haley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikki_Haley), the former South Carolina governor who served in [Trump's cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_cabinet_of_Donald_Trump), as the only remaining major candidates.[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132] Trump continued to win all four early voting contests while Haley's campaign struggled to gain momentum.[^cite_note-133] On March 6, 2024, the day after winning only one primary out of fifteen on [Super Tuesday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Super_Tuesday), Haley suspended her campaign.[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135] On March 12, 2024, Trump officially became the presumptive Republican presidential nominee.[^cite_note-136] Trump was injured in [an assassination attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attempted_assassination_of_Donald_Trump_in_Pennsylvania) on July 13, 2024, when a bullet grazed his ear.[^cite_note-CNNAssassinationUnfold-137] This was the first time a president or major party presidential candidate was injured in an assassination attempt since [Ronald Reagan in 1981](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attempted_assassination_of_Ronald_Reagan).[^cite_note-TownAndCountryAttempts-138] On July 15, 2024, the first day of the [Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Republican_National_Convention), Trump officially announced that Senator [JD Vance](53396477.md) of Ohio would be his running mate.[^cite_note-Hill-0715-139] On July 18, 2024, for the third consecutive time, Trump accepted the nomination from the [Republican National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_National_Convention) to become the Republican presidential nominee.[^cite_note-140] Trump is the first major party candidate to have been nominated by their party for three or more consecutive elections since [Franklin D. Roosevelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franklin_D._Roosevelt) in [1944](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1944_United_States_presidential_election).

### Democratic Party



Results of the 2024 Democratic presidential primaries. Biden (*blue*), as the presumptive nominee, won everything but [American Samoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Samoa), which went to [Jason Palmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jason_Palmer_\(politician\)) (*purple*).

On July 24, Biden addressed the nation from the [Oval Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oval_Office) on his decision three days earlier to [withdraw from the race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_Joe_Biden_from_the_2024_United_States_presidential_election).

Main articles: [2024 Democratic Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Democratic_Party_presidential_primaries) and [2024 Democratic Party presidential candidates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Democratic_Party_presidential_candidates)

On April 25, 2023, President [Joe Biden](145422.md) officially announced his [re-election campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Biden_2024_presidential_campaign), confirming that Vice President [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) would remain his running mate.[^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168]

[Concerns about Biden's age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_and_health_concerns_about_Joe_Biden) were prominent, given that he was [the oldest person to assume the office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_United_States_by_age) at age 78, which would make him 82 at the end of his first term and 86 at the end of a potential second term.[^cite_note-169] An April 2023 poll indicated that 70 percent of Americans, including 51 percent of Democrats, believed Biden should not seek a second term, with nearly half citing his age as the reason. Biden's approval rating stood at 41 percent, with 55 percent disapproving.[^cite_note-170] Speculation also arose that Biden might face a primary challenge from the [Democratic Party's progressive faction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Factions_in_the_Democratic_Party_\(United_States\)#Progressives);[^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172] however, after Democrats outperformed expectations in the [2022 midterm elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_States_elections), many believed Biden's chances of securing the party's nomination had increased.[^cite_note-173] On July 28, 2022, Representative [Dean Phillips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dean_Phillips) of [Minnesota](19590.md) became the first incumbent Democratic member of Congress to say Biden should not run for re-election and called for "generational change", pointing to Biden's age.[^cite_note-174][^cite_note-175]

Despite a handful of primary challengers, including Representative Dean Phillips, [Robert F. Kennedy Jr.](522298.md), [Marianne Williamson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marianne_Williamson), and [Jason Palmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jason_Palmer_\(politician\)), Biden easily became the party's presumptive nominee on March 12, 2024.[^cite_note-176][^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178] Palmer, who won the [American Samoa caucuses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_American_Samoa_Democratic_presidential_caucuses), became the first candidate to win a contested primary against an incumbent president since [Ted Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Kennedy) in 1980.[^cite_note-179] Biden also faced significant opposition from [uncommitted voters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncommitted_\(voting_option\)) and the [Uncommitted National Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncommitted_National_Movement) in their [protest vote movement against Biden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaza_war_protest_vote_movements) due to his support for Israel during the [Gaza war](74998519.md), which collectively won 36 delegates.[^cite_note-180]

Following a "disastrous" June 2024 [debate performance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Joe_Biden%E2%80%93Donald_Trump_presidential_debate) against Trump that "inflamed age concerns",[^cite_note-181] Biden ultimately [withdrew from the race](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_Joe_Biden_from_the_2024_United_States_presidential_election) on July 21, 2024, and immediately endorsed [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris) to replace him in his place as the party's presidential nominee.[^cite_note-wp-election-2024-102] Harris quickly announced [her own presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris_2024_presidential_campaign) later that day and by the next day, Harris had secured the non-binding support of enough uncommitted delegates that were previously pledged to Biden to make her the party's presumptive nominee.[^cite_note-182] Biden's withdrawal made him the first eligible incumbent president since [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) in [1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Withdrawal_of_Lyndon_B._Johnson_from_the_1968_United_States_presidential_election) not to seek re-election, and the first to withdraw after securing enough delegates to win the nomination.[^cite_note-183] Harris is the first presidential nominee who did not participate in the [presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_primary) since Vice President [Hubert Humphrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubert_Humphrey), also in [1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubert_Humphrey_1968_presidential_campaign), and the first since [the modern Democratic Party primary procedure was created in 1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McGovern%E2%80%93Fraser_Commission) (prior to which most states did not hold primary elections).[^cite_note-184]

On August 5, 2024, after five days of online balloting, [Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Democratic_National_Convention) delegates voted to make Harris the party's 2024 presidential nominee.[^cite_note-185] She [selected](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Democratic_Party_vice_presidential_candidate_selection) Minnesota Governor [Tim Walz](2216593.md) as her running mate the following day[^cite_note-186] and accepted the party's nomination on August 22.[^cite_note-187]

### Third-party and independent candidates

Main article: [Third-party and independent candidates for the 2024 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third-party_and_independent_candidates_for_the_2024_United_States_presidential_election)

A number of [independent candidates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_candidates) announced presidential runs, most notably [Robert F. Kennedy Jr.](522298.md) and [Cornel West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornel_West). Several third parties, including the [Libertarian Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarian_Party_\(United_States\)), the [Green Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Party_of_the_United_States), the [Party for Socialism and Liberation](42034851.md), the [Constitution Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_Party_\(United_States\)), and the [American Solidarity Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Solidarity_Party) also announced presidential nominees.[^cite_note-193] Kennedy dropped out of the race in August 2024, although he remained on the ballot in many states. The [No Labels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_Labels) organization abandoned its efforts to run a [centrist](39619035.md) candidate in April 2024.[^cite_note-194]

#### With majority ballot access

##### Libertarian Party

Main articles: [2024 Libertarian Party presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Libertarian_Party_presidential_primaries), [2024 Libertarian National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Libertarian_National_Convention), and [Chase Oliver 2024 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chase_Oliver_2024_presidential_campaign)

[Chase Oliver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chase_Oliver) was chosen by the [Libertarian Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libertarian_Party_\(United_States\)) as its presidential nominee on May 26, 2024, at the [2024 Libertarian National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Libertarian_National_Convention). Oliver was the party's nominee in the [2022 United States Senate election in Georgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_United_States_Senate_election_in_Georgia).[^cite_note-195] Oliver achieved [ballot access](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballot_access_in_the_2024_United_States_presidential_election) in 47 states, and was eligible to receive write-in votes in the District of Columbia, Illinois, New York, and Tennessee.[^cite_note-TGP_ballot_access-196][^cite_note-NY_write-in-197]
